{"stdout": "workspace/.terminal_claude_agent_struct_out.json:4:  \"paper_text\": \"# Over-Refusal of Benign Slovene Prompts Survives English De-censoring in Gemma-3-12B\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\nSafety-aligned language models sometimes refuse benign requests, and this over-refusal can vary across languages. We measure over-refusal in a controlled abliteration study comparing Gemma-3-12B-IT with its Slovene-adapted sibling GaMS3-12B-Instruct. Using signal-detection theory, we find that unedited Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at nearly twice the English rate --- a criterion shift with no corresponding change in sensitivity --- while GaMS3 shows no such language-dependent shift. This finding replicates across four independent experiments. A secondary analysis of reply-language refusal in a crossed prompt-language x reply-language design yields a raw output-language effect for Gemma, but the estimate is fragile: the prediction-powered-inference correction is consistent with zero and the adjudicator agrees with the primary judge at only kappa = 0.12 in Slovene. The results indicate that multilingual safety evaluations should measure false-alarm rates on benign items, not only attack success on harmful ones.\\n\\n## 1 Introduction\\n\\nSafety alignment in large language models typically targets harmful-request refusal, but the same training can also produce over-refusal: the model declines benign requests it should answer \\\\cite{Rottger2023, Souly2024}. Over-refusal degrades the model's usefulness, and prior work has shown that both refusal rates and over-refusal rates can differ across languages \\\\cite{Shen2024, Yong2023, Wang2023, Deng2023}. Whether these differences reflect a sensitivity gap (the model is less able to distinguish harmful from benign items) or a criterion shift (the model applies a stricter threshold for the same discriminative ability) has rarely been tested.\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig_overview]\\n\\nAbliteration --- directional ablation of the refusal direction in a model's residual stream \\\\cite{Arditi2024} --- provides a controlled way to study residual refusal after a partial English-objective edit. If the model's safety alignment is language-uniform, an English edit should reduce refusal equally in all languages. If it is not, the residual refusal after the edit reveals the structure of the asymmetry.\\n\\nWe compare two 12-billion-parameter models: Gemma-3-12B-IT \\\\cite{Wang2025} and GaMS3-12B-Instruct, a Slovene-adapted variant built on the same Gemma-3 base \\\\cite{Vres2026}. The abliteration edit is applied via per-layer LoRA adapters optimized by TPE \\\\cite{Akiba2019, Hu2021}. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_14]\\n\\nThe study addresses two questions. First, does the residual refusal after an English edit reflect a criterion shift (over-refusal on benign items) or a sensitivity difference (inability to discriminate)? Second, does the residual refusal track the language of the model's reply or the language of the prompt?\\n\\n### Summary of Contributions\\n\\n1. **Over-refusal as criterion shift (Section 4.1).** Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at nearly twice the English rate. Signal-detection analysis attributes this to a criterion shift (DiD_c = +0.52), not a sensitivity difference, replicated across four experiments. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_5]\\n2. **Reply-language refusal as an estimate (Section 4.2).** A crossed 3x3 design yields a raw output-language effect for Gemma, but prediction-powered inference and robustness checks weaken it to an estimate. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_16]\\n3. **Measurement methodology (Section 5).** We quantify how judge specificity, language compliance, and correction methods interact when evaluating multilingual refusal.\\n\\n## 2 Related Work\\n\\n**Multilingual refusal gaps.** Several studies document that safety alignment degrades in non-English languages, enabling jailbreaks via low-resource languages \\\\cite{Yong2023, Deng2023, Shen2024, Wang2023}. Recent work separates input-language from output-language effects \\\\cite{Marchisio2024, Ifebi2026, Aziz2026, Zhang2026} and shows that mechanistic safety pathways are shared across languages \\\\cite{Miao2026, Upadhyaya2026, Wang2025}. The question of whether multilingual refusal gaps reflect over-refusal (false alarms on benign items) rather than under-refusal (missed harmful items) is partially addressed by Addagada \\\\cite{Addagada2026} and Nguyen \\\\cite{Nguyen2026}, who observe language-dependent false-positive rates, making the novelty of the present work partially occupied.\\n\\n**Abliteration.** Arditi et al. \\\\cite{Arditi2024} showed that refusal in LLMs is mediated by a single direction in the residual stream, and that ablating this direction removes refusal. Joad et al. \\\\cite{Joad2026} demonstrated that multiple directions contribute, and Wang et al. \\\\cite{Wang2025} found that the refusal direction generalizes across safety-aligned languages. Krasnodebska et al. \\\\cite{Krasnodebska2026} studied multilingual refusal alignment. Hawkins et al. \\\\cite{Hawkins2026} examined the safety impacts of benign multilingual fine-tuning.\\n\\n**Evaluation methodology.** LLM-as-judge evaluations carry systematic biases that compound across languages \\\\cite{Lee2025, Vu2024}. Prediction-powered inference (PPI/PPI++) provides a framework for correcting classifier estimates against a gold standard \\\\cite{Angelopoulos2023, Angelopoulos2023a}. StrongREJECT \\\\cite{Souly2024} introduced rubric-based evaluation of jailbreak quality. Dahir \\\\cite{Dahir2026} measured English-to-Somali refusal gaps using similar methodology.\\n\\n## 3 Method\\n\\n### 3.1 Models and Edit Procedure\\n\\nWe study Gemma-3-12B-IT (rev 96b6f1ec) and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (rev 1d0b27af), both loaded in NF4 quantization. GaMS3 extends Gemma-3 with Slovene instruction tuning and DPO alignment \\\\cite{Vres2026}. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_9]\\n\\nThe abliteration edit applies per-layer directional ablation via LoRA adapters \\\\cite{Hu2021}, optimized by a 40-trial TPE search \\\\cite{Akiba2019}. Each trial selects a layer subset, computes a refusal direction from contrastive pairs of harmful and benign English prompts, and projects it out of the residual stream. The edit objective is to reduce English refusal while preserving capability on six benchmarks. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_12]\\n\\nWe use two edit doses throughout: a \\\"low\\\" dose (lambda = 0.5 for Gemma, 0.5 for GaMS3) and a \\\"high\\\" dose (lambda = 1.0 for Gemma, 0.625 for GaMS3), chosen to bracket the 50% English refusal point. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_15]\\n\\n### 3.2 Evaluation Design\\n\\n**Over-refusal (criterion shift).** Experiment 5 tests 1,093 safe items (XSTest \\\\cite{Rottger2023} and OR-Bench) and 349 harmful items in English and Slovene (machine-translated via NLLB \\\\cite{Costajussa2022}). Each item is classified as refused or complied by a distilled mdeBERTa judge. The hit rate (refusal on harmful items) and false-alarm rate (refusal on safe items) are computed per model x language cell, and signal-detection parameters d' (sensitivity) and c (criterion) are derived. The key contrast is the difference-in-differences: DiD_c = (c_Gemma_SL - c_Gemma_EN) - (c_GaMS3_SL - c_GaMS3_EN). [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_5]\\n\\n**Crossed grid (reply-language refusal).** Experiment 14 crosses three prompt languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) with three reply languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) in a 3x3 grid. Each cell contains 200 harmful items at three dose levels (zero, low, high). The output-language mechanism contrast OUT_SL is defined as the mean log-odds refusal gap between Slovene-output and English-output cells, averaging over prompt languages. The input-language contrast IN_SL is defined symmetrically. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_14]\\n\\n**Judges and corrections.** The primary judge is a distilled mdeBERTa classifier. Its accuracy is assessed against a Sonnet 4.5 adjudicator on 30 items per cell. We report the PPI++ correction \\\\cite{Angelopoulos2023a} alongside the raw estimate. Experiment 16 uses a held-out set of 294 items with a PPI estimator. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_16]\\n\\n**Language compliance.** Cells where fewer than 90% of model replies match the requested output language are excluded from mechanism claims or marked with a compliance flag. The excluded cells are: SL->EN (GaMS3 compliance 0.00), HU->EN (GaMS3 compliance 0.14), SL->HU (GaMS3 compliance 0.56), and HU->SL (Gemma compliance 0.96, GaMS3 compliance 0.86).\\n\\n### 3.3 Hungarian as a Control Language\\n\\nHungarian serves as a control language that is typologically distant from both English and Slovene. Experiment 11 established that Hungarian patterns are uninformative for the present questions: the minimum detectable effect for Hungarian contrasts is approximately 3 log-odds units, far larger than any observed effect. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_11]\\n\\n## 4 Results\\n\\n### 4.1 Over-Refusal: Criterion Shift on Benign Items\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig_sdt]\\n\\nThe central finding is that Gemma applies a stricter refusal criterion in Slovene than in English when processing benign items, while GaMS3 does not. Table 1 reports the signal-detection parameters from Experiment 5.\\n\\n**Table 1: Signal-detection parameters by model and language (Experiment 5, n = 1,093 safe + 349 harmful items)**\\n\\n| | Gemma EN | Gemma SL | GaMS3 EN | GaMS3 SL |\\n|---|---|---|---|---|\\n| Hit rate (H) | 0.89 | 0.94 | 0.90 | 0.86 |\\n| False-alarm rate (F) | 0.364 | 0.569 | 0.273 | 0.273 |\\n| d' | 1.57 | 1.39 | 1.86 | 1.68 |\\n| c | -0.44 | -0.87 | -0.33 | -0.23 |\\n\\nThe DiD on the criterion parameter is +0.52 [0.38, 0.68] (95% CI), indicating that Gemma's Slovene criterion is shifted by half a standard deviation relative to the GaMS3 baseline. The DiD on d' is -0.003 [-0.33, 0.29], indistinguishable from zero: the models do not differ in how the language shift affects their ability to discriminate harmful from benign items. The asymmetry is entirely in the threshold, not the sensitivity.\\n\\n**Replication.** The criterion-shift finding replicates in three further experiments. Experiment 14 yields a matching SDT contrast (hi dose, SL-output cells: DiD_c = -1.56 [-1.97, -1.29], Gemma minus GaMS3). Experiment 16 provides an independent held-out confirmation. Evaluation 4 re-judges the same items with Qwen3-14B as an independent adjudicator, yielding DiD_c in [0.39, 0.67]. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_evaluation_4]\\n\\n### 4.2 Reply-Language Refusal (ESTIMATE)\\n\\nIn the 3x3 crossed grid (Experiment 14), Gemma at high edit dose shows a pattern in which residual refusal is higher when the model replies in Slovene than when it replies in English, regardless of prompt language. The raw mechanism contrast is OUT_SL = +1.14 [0.60, 1.56] log-odds for Gemma, and the input-language contrast is IN_SL = -0.60 [-1.02, -0.18]. For GaMS3, the input-language contrast is IN_SL = -0.86 [-1.58, -0.13], which excludes zero.\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig_grid]\\n\\n**Table 2: Refusal rates at high edit dose, Gemma (harmful items, 200 per cell). Cells with <90% language compliance are marked with \\\\dag.**\\n\\n| | EN out | SL out | HU out |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| EN in | 0.18 | 0.77 | 0.46 |\\n| SL in | 0.07 | 0.56 | 0.53 |\\n| HU in | 0.10 | 0.83 | 0.55 |\\n\\n**Table 3: Refusal rates at high edit dose, GaMS3 (harmful items, 200 per cell). Cells with <90% language compliance are marked with \\\\dag.**\\n\\n| | EN out | SL out | HU out |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| EN in | 0.21 | 0.14 | 0.23 |\\n| SL in | 0.16\\\\dag | 0.15 | 0.17\\\\dag |\\n| HU in | 0.19\\\\dag | 0.12\\\\dag | 0.28 |\\n\\nThis finding is rated ESTIMATE for three reasons. First, the PPI-corrected output-language contrast for Gemma is 0.20 [-0.76, 1.13], consistent with zero. Second, the pre-registered four-readout robustness gate yields robust = False for the Slovene output-language call. Third, the adjudicator achieves kappa = 0.12 with the primary judge in the Gemma Slovene-edited cell (Se = 0.927, Sp = 0.714, n = 48), indicating that the judge-specificity anchor is weak. Experiment 16 provides a confirmatory point estimate (OUT_Gemma = +2.43 [+0.89, +5.38] on a held-out 294-item set), but the judge-specificity concern persists.\\n\\n**Compliant-row contrast.** Restricting to rows where both models achieve >90% compliance (EN-input rows only), the edit-induced refusal lag in the EN->SL cell is -1.26 [-2.17, 0.20], which includes zero.\\n\\n### 4.3 Cross-Model Specificity\\n\\nThe cross-model contrasts test whether Gemma's pattern differs from GaMS3's. The output-language specificity contrast is dOUT_SL = -1.58 [-2.22, -0.66] (MDE 1.07), rated DIFFERENT: Gemma's output-language effect is larger than GaMS3's. The input-language specificity contrast is dIN_SL = -0.26 [-1.13, 0.57] (MDE 1.19), rated ESTIMATE: the two models do not measurably differ in input-language sensitivity.\\n\\n### 4.4 Refusal Composition\\n\\nAmong Gemma's Slovene refusals at high dose (eval4 adjudication, n = 30 per cell), the rate of any refusal is 0.20 [0.10, 0.37] for GaMS3 EN-output versus 0.10 [0.03, 0.26] for GaMS3 SL-output. Of Gemma's Slovene refusals, 54% are explicit (the model states that it cannot help) and 26% are deflections (the model redirects the conversation without directly refusing). [ARTIFACT:gen_art_evaluation_4]\\n\\n### 4.5 Dose-Response\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig_dose]\\n\\nExperiment 9 measures refusal rates across nine lambda values for both models in English and Slovene (300 items per condition). Gemma's dose-response curve in Slovene is right-shifted relative to its English curve: at matched doses, more Slovene refusal remains. GaMS3 shows a smaller and non-significant shift. The 13-step dense dose ladder (Experiment 15) confirms that the refusal gap is present across the dose range, not concentrated at a single operating point. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_9]\\n\\n### 4.6 Inconclusive and Null Results\\n\\n**G3_orig (unedited GaMS3 vs Gemma in SL->SL).** The unedited GaMS3 shows a refusal gap of -1.17 [-2.83, 0.22] relative to Gemma in the SL->SL cell. This estimate is inconclusive: it includes zero, its fragility index is 1 (a single item reclassification would flip the sign), and the unedited models operate near ceiling refusal rates (Gemma 0.95, GaMS3 0.87), making log-odds contrasts unreliable.\\n\\n**Hungarian.** All Hungarian contrasts are uninformative. The minimum detectable effect for Hungarian is approximately 3 log-odds units, and no observed Hungarian effect exceeds this threshold.\\n\\n**RefusEU validation.** Experiment 17 tests ASR on the reserved RefusEU benchmark (1,300 EN + 1,300 SL items). The DiD on this benchmark is inconclusive (MDE > 2m), consistent with the near-ceiling refusal rates on this corpus. [ARTIFACT:gen_art_experiment_17]\\n\\n## 5 Discussion\\n\\n### 5.1 Interpretation\\n\\nThe over-refusal finding has a concrete interpretation for multilingual evaluation: a model can appear \\\"safer\\\" in a non-English language simply because it refuses more benign requests, inflating the refusal rate without any improvement in harm detection. The signal-detection framing separates these two components. The near-zero DiD on d' means that the language shift does not make either model better or worse at distinguishing harmful from benign items; the entire difference is in the threshold.\\n\\nThe reply-language finding, if confirmed by future work with stronger judge anchors, would suggest that the refusal mechanism is partially encoded in the model's output-language circuitry rather than the input-language pathway. This is consistent with mechanistic findings that safety-related representations are entangled with language-identity features \\\\cite{Upadhyaya2026, Miao2026}.\\n\\n### 5.2 Limitations\\n\\n**Sample size.** The adjudication sample is 30 items per cell, which limits the precision of judge-validity estimates. The false-alarm rate estimates rest on 1,093 safe items, adequate for the pooled contrast but noisy at the per-category level.\\n\\n**Judge specificity.** The primary judge (distilled mdeBERTa) achieves Sp = 0.714 in the Gemma Slovene-edited cell, meaning roughly 29% of non-refusals are misclassified as refusals. This inflates the raw refusal rate and weakens the reply-language finding. The PPI correction addresses this statistically but depends on the gold-label quality of the Sonnet 4.5 adjudicator, which is itself an LLM.\\n\\n**Language compliance.** Four cells (SL->EN, HU->EN, SL->HU, HU->SL) fall below 90% compliance, limiting the crossed-grid analysis to compliant rows for mechanism claims.\\n\\n**Quantization.** Both models run in NF4 quantization, which introduces noise relative to full-precision inference. The abliteration edit is applied to the quantized model, and the interaction between quantization and directional ablation is not characterized.\\n\\n**Novelty.** The observation that over-refusal rates differ across languages is partially anticipated by Addagada \\\\cite{Addagada2026} and Nguyen \\\\cite{Nguyen2026}. The present work's contribution is the signal-detection decomposition (criterion vs. sensitivity) and the controlled abliteration design, not the observation itself.\\n\\n## 6 Conclusion\\n\\nUnedited Gemma-3-12B-IT over-refuses benign Slovene prompts at a rate of 0.569, compared to 0.364 in English. Signal-detection analysis attributes this entirely to a criterion shift (DiD_c = +0.52 [0.38, 0.68]) with no sensitivity difference, replicated across four experiments. A secondary analysis suggests that residual refusal under partial abliteration tracks the reply language, but this finding is rated ESTIMATE due to weak judge anchoring (kappa = 0.12) and a PPI correction consistent with zero. Multilingual safety evaluations that report only attack-success rates on harmful items will miss criterion shifts that inflate refusal rates on benign items.\\n\\n\\\\bibliography{references}\\n\\\\bibliographystyle{plainnat}\",\nworkspace/.terminal_claude_agent_struct_out.json-11-      \"image_gen_detailed_description\": \"A concept diagram showing the experimental pipeline. {"stdout": "invisible to a reviewer of the prompt and obvious to a reviewer of the\npaper.\n\n## Use a generator when one fits — hand-write only when none does\n\nThe generators are a menu, not a fence. Every type below is a shortcut that\nalready has the house style, the data-integrity guards and the layout fixes\nbaked in, so reaching for one is almost always less work than plotting by\nhand and the result is consistent with every other figure in the paper.\n\n**Check `--list-types` first.** If a type matches what you need, use it.\nDon't know the name? `--search \"<the question your figure answers>\"` ranks\nthe catalogue by intent rather than by name — `--search \"before and after\nper method\"` puts `slope` first and `dumbbell` second.\nTwo-thirds of research figures are a bar, a line, a scatter or a heatmap,\nand those are solved.\n\n`--search` spans **two corpora** and labels every hit with which one it\ncame from:\n\n| label | what it is | what to do |\n|---|---|---|\n| `ours: <type>` | one of our 61 types | `--example`, edit, render |\n| `chartmimic: <task>/<id>` | a published figure | read its `.py` |\n\nA `chartmimic:` hit is a **reference, not a spec.** It is a human-curated\nfigure from a STEM paper with the matplotlib that draws it — from\nChartMimic ([arXiv:2406.09961](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.09961)), 4,800 of\nthem over 22 categories. Adapting one is a *hand-written* figure: no house\nstyle, no data-integrity guards, no layout passes unless you call them, so\neverything above about hand-written figures still applies. The search\nprints the path to its code under every such hit. Generators outrank\nexemplars on a tie, because a generator is the runnable answer.\n\nReach for an exemplar in exactly two cases: **nothing in the catalogue\nfits** (see the gap table below), or you want to see how a published figure\ndid something — a twin axis, a labelled contour — in working code.\n`--corpus ours|chartmimic|all` narrows the search; the default is `all`.\n\n**If nothing fits, write matplotlib yourself** — that is expected and\nsupported, not a failure. Novel or one-off figures exist. When you do:\n\n```python\nimport sys; sys.path.insert(0, \"<skill>/scripts\")\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nfrom chart_geometry import assert_text_is_legible, fit_point_labels\nfrom chart_style import (\n    apply_house_style, PALETTE, literal, place_legend, place_point_label,\n    fit_legends, clear_legends_of_data, fit_tick_labels, fit_titles,\n    rasterize_dense_clouds, assert_legends_clear_of_data,\n    assert_series_are_distinguishable, assert_axis_names_are_unique,\n)\n\napply_house_style()                 # fonts, palette, grid, Type-42 PDF fonts\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 3.94), layout=\"constrained\")\n...\nplace_legend(ax, loc=\"best\")        # a legend fit_legends can reflow\nplace_point_label(ax, literal(\"Ours\"), (1, 2))   # a name, nudged off the data\nfit_legends(fig)                    # reflow a legend wider than its axes\nclear_legends_of_data(fig)          # move it below the axes if it sits on data\nfit_tick_labels(fig)                # wrap/tilt tick labels that would collide\nfit_titles(fig)                     # wrap any title wider than its axes\nclear_legends_of_data(fig)          # AGAIN — the two above reshaped the axes\nfit_point_labels(fig)               # move point names off markers and curves\nrasterize_dense_clouds(fig)         # >25k points as a bitmap, text stays vector\nassert_text_is_legible(fig)         # raises if any text collides or is cut off\nassert_legends_clear_of_data(fig)   # raises if a legend still hides its data\nassert_series_are_distinguishable(fig)  # raises on two identical legend keys\nassert_axis_names_are_unique(fig)   # raises if one name labels two positions\nfig.savefig(\"figX_v0.pdf\")          # vector, so LaTeX renders text at page res\n```\n\nCall the fitters in that order — the legend decides how much room the axes\nhas, whether it then has to move out of the data is only knowable once it is\nplaced, tick labels change the axes height, the title is measured against the\naxes it ends up on, and a point's name can only be placed once nothing above\nit will move the point again. `clear_legends_of_data` appears TWICE on\npurpose: it decides by measuring, and the two passes between its calls shrink\nthe axes under a legend that is already placed and a fixed size. A wrapped\ntitle took a lone chart from 179 px of axes height to 141, and a legend that\ncovered nothing before covered half a curve after — with the mover's turn\nalready past, so the figure was refused rather than fixed. The first call\nstill has to happen first, because the room the legend needs is an input to\nthe passes below it. Two further gates are warning-based and so are\nnot in the snippet: `assert_layout_applied` and `assert_all_glyphs_rendered`\nread what matplotlib warned about during the draw, so they need the figure\nbuilt inside `warnings.catch_warnings(record=True)` — worth doing, since a\nmissing glyph is only ever a warning and ships as a hollow box.\n`place_legend` and `place_point_label` are how\nthe fitters find what to fix: a legend built with a bare `ax.legend` cannot\nbe reflowed, and a name written with a bare `ax.annotate` will not be moved\noff the marker it landed on.\n\nThat keeps a hand-written figure looking like the rest of the paper and\nstill gets you colourblind-safe colours, submission-compliant fonts, no\nclipped labels and no overprinted ones. What you lose is the data-integrity\nchecking — so verify the numbers yourself.\n\n**If you hand-write the same figure type twice, add a renderer instead.**\n`chart_renderers*.py` — one function, `(ax, spec) -> None`, registered in\nits family's dict. That is how this catalogue got here.\n\n## Use it\n\n```bash\nSKILL_DIR=\"$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel 2>/dev/null || echo /ai-inventor)/.claude/skills/aii-data-fig-gen\"\nG=\"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/chart_gen.py\"\n\npython \"$G\" --list-types            # the catalogue\npython \"$G\" --search \"compare distributions across groups\"   # find it by intent\npython \"$G\" --search \"pie wedges\" --corpus chartmimic         # exemplars only\npython \"$G\" --audit                 # what ChartMimic has that we do not\npython \"$G\" --example bar           # a complete spec to copy and edit\npython \"$G\" --spec fig1.json --out figures/fig1\n```\n\n`python` here is the pipeline image's interpreter, which has matplotlib and\nscipy installed system-wide. Outside the image use the project venv —\n`.venv/bin/python` — since a bare `python3` will not have them.\n\nWrites `figures/fig1.pdf` **and** `figures/fig1.png`. The PDF is the\ndeliverable — LaTeX renders vector text at page resolution, so it stays\nsharp and selectable at any zoom. The PNG exists so you can read the figure\nback and look at it.\n\n`--format pdf`, `--format png`, `--format pdf,png,svg` narrows the output.\nSVG keeps its labels as TEXT rather than paths, so it stays editable and\nsearchable. EPS is refused: the PostScript backend cannot draw transparency\nand flattens it silently, which the house style uses on nine of every ten\nfigures — the file would not match the PNG you checked.\n`--spec -` reads the spec from stdin.\n\nRuns on `matplotlib` + `numpy`, both already `aii_pipeline` dependencies —\nnothing to install.\n\n## The catalogue\n\n`--example <type>` prints a complete spec for any of these. The \"choose it\nover\" half of each entry is the useful one: most figures have two plausible\ntypes and the choice between them is what decides whether a reviewer reads\nthe point.\n\n### Comparing categories\n\n- `bar` — draws: Vertical bars, grouped or stacked, optional error bars.\n  Choose it over: The default. `barh` if names are long.\n- `barh` — draws: Horizontal bars — labels on the y-axis with room to run.\n  Choose it over: `bar`, whenever names exceed ~40 chars, or for a ranking.\n- `lollipop` — draws: A stem and a dot per category. Choose it over: `barh`,\n  past ~20 categories, where bars become a picket fence.\n- `dumbbell` — draws: Two markers per row joined by a line. Choose it over:\n  Paired bars, when the GAP between them is the story.\n- `slope` — draws: One line per item from a before value to an after value.\n  Choose it over: Paired bars, when which items changed RANK is the story.\n- `bump` — draws: Rank against time, one line per item; the crossings are\n  the finding. Choose it over: `slope`, which shows a reordering for exactly\n  TWO time points and cannot show the path between more.\n- `volcano` — draws: Effect size against significance, with both thresholds\n  drawn. Choose it over: A `bar` of effects, which cannot show what survived\n  correction, or a table of p-values, which cannot show what was big enough\n  to matter.\n- `diverging` — draws: Signed bars either side of zero, sorted. Choose it\n  over: `bar`, for deltas — direction reads instantly.\n- `waterfall` — draws: Steps from a starting total to a final total. Choose\n  it over: `bar`, for an ablation — it shows contributions compounding.\n- `bar_sig` — draws: Grouped bars with significance brackets and stars.\n  Choose it over: `bar`, when the comparison being claimed is pairwise.\n- `forest` — draws: Point estimates with confidence intervals and a null\n  line. Choose it over: `bar`, when whether an interval crosses zero is the\n  question.\n- `radar` — draws: A closed polygon per method over 3+ metrics. Choose it\n  over: Several bar charts, for a multi-metric profile at a glance.\n- `parallel` — draws: One polyline per configuration across independently\n  scaled axes. Choose it over: A table, for a hyperparameter sweep — trends\n  across axes show up.\n- `funnel` — draws: Stage attrition with retention vs. previous and vs.\n  intake. Choose it over: `barh`, when the stages are sequential and losses\n  compound.\n- `stacked_pct` — draws: Composition as percentages; every bar full height.\n  Choose it over: Stacked `bar`, when categories have very different totals.\n- `treemap` — draws: Nested rectangles with AREA proportional to value.\n  Choose it over: `bar`, only when there are too many parts for one axis —\n  length beats area for precise reading.\n- `upset` — draws: Set intersections as sorted bars over a membership\n  matrix. Choose it over: A Venn diagram, past 3 sets — circles cannot stay\n  area-true and stop reading as sets.\n\n### Trends and relationships\n\n- `line` — draws: Multi-series lines with optional uncertainty bands. Choose\n  it over: The default for anything against time or steps.\n- `fan` — draws: A median with nested quantile bands around it. Choose it\n  over: `line` with a band, when the spread is skewed or bounded — a\n  symmetric ± band on an accuracy near its ceiling implies scores above\n  100%.\n- `step` — draws: A piecewise-constant series — value holds, then jumps.\n  Choose it over: `line`, for schedules — a slope implies values that never\n  occurred.\n- `scatter` — draws: Points with an optional least-squares fit and R².\n  Choose it over: `line`, when x is not ordered and the relationship is the\n  point.\n- `joint` — draws: Scatter with the marginal distribution of each variable\n  beside it. Choose it over: `scatter`, when \"and how is each one\n  distributed?\" is the obvious next question — which for a headline\n  correlation it always is.\n- `splom` — draws: Every pair of variables as its own scatter, distributions\n  down the diagonal. Choose it over: `corr`, when the SHAPE of each\n  relationship is the claim — one number cannot tell a straight line from\n  two clusters or an outlier.\n- `bubble` — draws: Scatter with a third variable as marker AREA, plus a\n  size key. Choose it over: `scatter`, when a third quantity matters but not\n  enough for its own axis.\n- `scaling` — draws: Log-log points with a fitted power law and its\n  exponent. Choose it over: `line`, for scaling laws — the exponent is\n  computed and annotated.\n- `speedup` — draws: Measured speedup against worker count, with the ideal\n  line. Choose it over: `line`, for parallel results — the ideal reference\n  is what the claim is measured against.\n- `pareto` — draws: Scatter with the non-dominated frontier drawn through\n  it. Choose it over: `scatter`, for trade-offs where the frontier is the\n  finding.\n- `area` — draws: Stacked areas — a total and how it divides. Choose it\n  over: `line`, when the total matters as much as the parts.\n- `residual` — draws: Residuals against fitted values, with the zero line.\n  Choose it over: Predicted-vs-actual, where heteroscedasticity hides on the\n  diagonal.\n- `bland_altman` — draws: Difference between two methods against their mean,\n  with limits of agreement. Choose it over: A scatter of A against B, where\n  the diagonal reads as agreement and r = 0.99 hides a 10% offset.\n- `acf` — draws: Autocorrelation per lag as stems, with the significance\n  band. Choose it over: `line`, which shows the level and hides whether each\n  point predicts the next.\n- `sankey` — draws: Flows between stages at proportional widths. Choose it\n  over: `area`, when what matters is what became what.\n- `timeline` — draws: Gantt-style spans, one row per task. Choose it over: A\n  table of timestamps, when overlap and duration are the point.\n\n### Model evaluation\n\nGive these raw `labels` and `scores` rather than a precomputed curve wherever\nyou can: the renderer sweeps the threshold itself, so the AUC or AP in the\nlegend is integrated from the points actually drawn and cannot drift from\nthe curve beside it.\n\nWhen only the curve survives — it came from a paper, or from a logged\nartefact — pass it directly instead: `fpr`/`tpr` for `roc`, `recall`/\n`precision` for `pr`, `probabilities`/`labels` for `calibration`. The\nsummary statistic is still integrated from the plotted points, so a PR curve\nthat stops short reports `AP = 0.375 up to recall 0.60` rather than quietly\nextrapolating the rest. One evaluation set per figure: `pr`'s baseline and\n`calibration`'s bins both move with class balance, so curves from different\ntest sets cannot share axes honestly.\n\n- `roc` — draws: ROC curves with AUC in the legend, plus the chance\n  diagonal. Choose it over: `pr`, when the classes are roughly balanced.\n- `pr` — draws: Precision-recall curves with average precision and the\n  prevalence baseline. Choose it over: `roc`, when positives are rare — ROC\n  flatters a rare-class model.\n- `calibration` — draws: Reliability diagram with the ideal diagonal, ECE,\n  and per-bin counts. Choose it over: `roc`/`pr`, when whether to TRUST a\n  probability is the question.\n- `learning_curve` — draws: Score against training-set size, train and\n  validation with ±std bands. Choose it over: `line`, to show whether more\n  data or a better model is the bottleneck.\n- `qq` — draws: Sample quantiles against theoretical normal quantiles, with\n  a reference line. Choose it over: `hist`, for judging normality — the eye\n  reads a straight line far better than a bell.\n- `cd_diagram` — draws: Mean ranks over many datasets, joining methods a\n  test cannot separate. Choose it over: `bar_sig`, which compares pairwise\n  on ONE dataset — this is the many-datasets headline figure.\n\n### Distributions\n\n- `box` — draws: Median, quartiles, whiskers, outliers per group. Choose it\n  over: The compact default for a few groups.\n- `violin` — draws: Full mirrored density per group. Choose it over: `box`,\n  when a distribution may be multi-modal — a box hides that.\n- `strip` — draws: Every raw observation, jittered, with the mean marked.\n  Choose it over: `box`, when n is small enough that each point should be\n  visible.\n- `beeswarm` — draws: Every observation, packed sideways so none hides\n  another. Choose it over: `strip`, whose random jitter still overlaps at\n  any real n — the eye reads the clumps as density and they are partly\n  collision.\n- `ridgeline` — draws: Stacked density curves, one row per group. Choose it\n  over: `violin`, past ~6 groups, where a violin grid gets too wide.\n- `raincloud` — draws: Half violin, box and jittered points together, with\n  n. Choose it over: `violin`, when the reader must see the observations —\n  twelve seeds look as smooth as twelve thousand.\n- `hist` — draws: Binned counts or density. Choose it over: `ecdf`, only\n  when the shape of ONE distribution is the point.\n- `ecdf` — draws: Empirical cumulative distribution, stepped. Choose it\n  over: `hist`, for comparing distributions — no bin width to argue about.\n- `survival` — draws: Kaplan-Meier curves with censoring ticks and\n  confidence bands. Choose it over: `ecdf`, when some subjects have not\n  finished — an ECDF must drop or invent those.\n- `hexbin` — draws: Hexagonal density bins with a colourbar. Choose it over:\n  `scatter`, past ~2000 points where it becomes a solid blob.\n- `hist2d` — draws: A joint distribution as a rectangular binned grid.\n  Choose it over: `hexbin`, when the axes are naturally rectangular.\n\n### Matrices and fields\n\n- `heatmap` — draws: Annotated matrix with a colourbar. Choose it over: A\n  table, when the pattern matters more than the digits.\n- `seqheat` — draws: A per-token quantity drawn on the tokens themselves.\n  Choose it over: `heatmap`, for anything measured per token — it puts\n  indices on an axis and leaves the reader rebuilding the sentence from a\n  legend.\n- `corr` — draws: Correlation matrix, diverging map centred at zero. Choose\n  it over: `heatmap`, for correlations — sign reads from colour direction.\n- `contour` — draws: Filled contours of a 2-D field, levels labelled. Choose\n  it over: `heatmap`, for a smooth field like a loss surface.\n- `clustermap` — draws: Heatmap with rows and columns reordered into their\n  clusters, trees drawn beside. Choose it over: `heatmap`, whenever the row\n  order is arbitrary — block structure that is obvious once reordered is\n  invisible in the order the log happened to emit.\n- `catmap` — draws: A grid whose cells hold a CATEGORY, with a discrete\n  legend and no scale. Choose it over: `heatmap`, for any nominal cell —\n  expert IDs, pass/fail/timeout, which variant won. A ramp asserts that\n  expert 4 is more than expert 1 and that 2 lies between them, and a reader\n  takes the ordering as real.\n- `quiver` — draws: A field of arrows: where each sample is, and where it\n  went. Choose it over: A `scatter` of the before and after positions, which\n  carries the same numbers and leaves the reader pairing points up by eye.\n\n### Structure\n\n- `dendrogram` — draws: Hierarchical clustering as a tree, branch heights\n  the real merge distances. Choose it over: `corr`, which shows every\n  pairwise relationship and no grouping.\n- `tree` — draws: A rooted tree from a parent/child structure you already\n  have. Choose it over: `dendrogram`, which computes its own linkage from a\n  matrix and cannot be given a tree — and `network`, whose force layout\n  loses depth.\n- `network` — draws: A graph as nodes and links, node area and edge width\n  from the data. Choose it over: A concept figure, for anything with REAL\n  edges — an image model draws a plausible graph, not yours. Use `sankey`\n  for flows between ordered stages and `heatmap` for a dense graph.\n\n### Composites\n\n- `panel` — draws: Any of the above in a lettered grid, `(a)`–`(p)`. Choose\n  it over: Several separate figures, when they are read together.\n\n## What ChartMimic has that we do not\n\nMeasured, not guessed: `chart_gen.py --audit` maps all 22 ChartMimic\ncategories onto our 61 types over the 4,800 indexed exemplars. Seventeen\ncategories are covered. These five are not — every one of them is a figure\nshape real papers publish and no generator can produce:\n\n| ChartMimic | n | what to do instead |\n|---|---|---|\n| Combination | 240 | Hand-write. Bars + a line, usually `twinx`. |\n| Hard-to-Recognize | 200 | Hand-write; it is their catch-all. |\n| 3D | 160 | `heatmap` or `contour` — a surface hides data. |\n| Plot-in-Plot | 160 | Hand-write `ax.inset_axes`; not `panel`. |\n| Pie | 160 | `barh`, `stacked_pct` or `treemap`. |\n\n**Combination is the one real gap.** Bars with a line on a second y-axis is\na standard results figure and we have no type for it; the other four are\neither deliberate refusals (pie, 3-D — both read worse than what we do\nhave) or not a chart type at all (Hard-to-Recognize). Adding a\n`bar_line`/dual-axis renderer would close the largest measured hole in the\ncatalogue.\n\nThe 17 covered categories are where an exemplar is a REFERENCE rather than\na gap-filler: our generator is still the answer, and the exemplar shows how\na published figure handled the same shape.", "stderr": "", "interrupted": false, "isImage": false, "noOutputExpected": false}

Ordered so\n# the first three — the most common series count — are maximally separated:\n# ΔE*ab 52-69 apart across normal, protanopia and deuteranopia.\nPALETTE: tuple[str, ...] = (\n    \"#0173B2\",  # blue\n    \"#DE8F05\",  # amber\n    \"#029E73\",  # green\n    \"#CC78BC\",  # violet\n    \"#CA9161\",  # tan\n    \"#949494\",  # grey\n    \"#ECE133\",  # yellow\n    \"#56B4E9\",  # sky\n)\n\n# Dash patterns for when the palette wraps. Past eight series the colour\n# repeats exactly — series 1 and 9 were pixel-identical, which makes a legend\n# unusable — so the line style becomes the second channel that tells them\n# apart. It is also the only channel that survives greyscale print past the\n# third series, where the palette's lightnesses start to cluster.\nLINE_STYLES: tuple[str, ...] = (\"-\", \"--\", \"-.\", \":\")\n\n\ndef series_style(index: int) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"Colour, and past the palette's length a dash pattern too.\"\"\"\n    style = {\"color\": PALETTE[index % len(PALETTE)]}\n    if index >= len(PALETTE):\n        style[\"linestyle\"] = LINE_STYLES[(index // len(PALETTE)) % len(LINE_STYLES)]\n    return style\n\n\n# Sequential map for heatmaps: perceptually uniform AND colourblind-safe,\n# unlike the jet/rainbow maps that still show up in papers.\nSEQUENTIAL_CMAP = \"cividis\"\n# Diverging map for signed quantities (deltas, correlations).\nDIVERGING_CMAP = \"RdBu_r\"\n\n# Base font size in points. Figures are drawn at their final print size, so\n# this is what the reader actually sees — not a value scaled later.\nBASE_FONT_PT = 11\n\ndef figsize_for(aspect: str, width_in: float = 7.0) -> tuple[float, float]:\n    \"\"\"Figure size in inches for an ``W:H`` aspect string.\n\n    Width defaults to 7 inches — a full text-width figure at close to 100%\n    scale, which is the size the reader sees.\n\n    The generated size is deliberately NOT capped by height here. Capping it\n    to the paper's float limit was tried and is worse: a 1:1 figure comes out\n    3.6 x 3.6 in, a 2x2 panel gets 2.4 in per cell, and the legibility gates\n    then refuse figures that used to draw — 18 checks and two catalogue\n    examples went red. The shrink that motivated it belongs to the LaTeX\n    include, and is fixed there.\n    \"\"\"\n    # No fallback here. `validate_spec` refuses a malformed or non-positive\n    # aspect before this runs — measured against ten spellings (\"16x9\", \"1:0\",\n    # \"-16:9\", \":\", \"\" and the rest) down every route in: top-level, on a\n    # panel, on a panel's child, absent, and explicitly null. Not one reached\n    # this function; the only value that arrives is a parsed, positive pair.\n    #\n    # What used to sit here caught the parse failure and returned 16:9, which\n    # is the defect `test_an_aspect_that_cannot_be_parsed_is_refused_not_\n    # quietly_replaced` was written for: \"16x9\" drew the shape that was wanted\n    # by luck and \"4x3\" drew a 16:9 figure at exit 0, under a caption written\n    # for the other shape. A second copy of that fallback below the gate would\n    # restore exactly that behaviour on any path that ever skipped the gate,\n    # which is the last place it should come back.\n    w, h = (float(part) for part in aspect.split(\":\"))\n    return (width_in, width_in * h / w)\n\n\ndef literal(text) -> str:\n    \"\"\"User text, with ``$`` neutralised so matplotlib prints it verbatim.\ndef add_panel_label(ax, label: str) -> None:\n    \"\"\"Put a bold ``(a)``-style label above a subplot's top-left corner.\n\n    This uses matplotlib's own LEFT title slot rather than a free-floating\n    text artist. Two placements were tried first and both overprinted the\n    heading: prefixing it onto the title gave ``(d)Row-normalised confusion\n    matrix``, and a separate artist at the axes' top-left corner gave\n    ``Accurac(a)y by benchmark`` as soon as ``fit_titles`` grew the centred\n    title out to the full width of the cell.\n\n    An axes owns three independent title slots — left, centre and right —\n    laid out on one line by the same code that positions the heading. Giving\n    the label the left slot means the two are placed against each other by\n    matplotlib instead of by arithmetic here, so the ordering of these calls\n    stops mattering: the label may be attached before or after the title.\n    ``fit_titles`` reads this slot's width back and wraps the heading clear\n    of it.\n    \"\"\"\n    ax.set_title(label, loc=\"left\", fontweight=\"bold\")\n\n\ndef fix_log_ticks(ax, which: str) -> None:", "stderr": "\nShell cwd was reset to /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_UESxYRggGt7E/4_gen_paper_repo/_3_gen_viz/gen_viz_report_6", "interrupted": false, "isImage": false, "noOutputExpected": false}

### [152] THINKING · 2026-09-25 08:18:17 UTC

```
dOUT_SL isn't derived directly from the hi-dose rates—it comes from edit-induced lags (L*) across the EN/SL 2×2, with GaMS3's arm using the SL→EN cell that failed compliance. I'll check how `a` and L* are defined to make sure the caption reflects this accurately.
```

### [158] TOOL CALL — Write · 2026-09-25 08:18:51 UTC

```
File: /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_UESxYRggGt7E/4_gen_paper_repo/_3_gen_viz/gen_viz_report_6/build_spec.py

"""Build fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json from the Experiment 14 artifact's saved outputs.

Experiment 14 (invention-loop iteration 4, `gen_art_experiment_14`) crossed prompt language
(EN, SL, HU) with requested reply language (EN, SL, HU) on 200 harmful items per cell, for
Gemma-3-12B-IT and GaMS3-12B-Instruct at three edit doses (zero, lo, hi). This script plots the
hi dose only (lambda_hi = 1.0 for Gemma, 0.625 for GaMS3, read from results/lambda_steps_*.json).

Reads, from the artifact directory:
  - results/rows_final.jsonl   : one row per response; `L_primary` is the primary judge's label
  - results/analysis.json      : cell_table (stored rates and Wilson CIs), compliance_gate,
                                 readouts.raw_R (dOUT_SL, G3edit|ensl, OUT_SL per model),
                                 robust_call_SL
  - results/lambda_steps_<model>.json : the lambda value of the hi dose

For every one of the 18 plotted cells it recounts k/n from rows_final.jsonl (harmful, cond=edit,
dose=hi; R = share of labelled rows with L_primary == REFUSE), recomputes the Wilson 95% interval,
and ASSERTS both match analysis.json cell_table. Nothing is copied from the paper draft.

Usage: python build_spec.py <exp14-artifact-dir> [--out fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json]
"""
import argparse
import json
import math
from collections import defaultdict
from pathlib import Path

LANGS = ["en", "sl", "hu"]
MODELS = [("gemma_it", "Gemma-IT"), ("gams3_it", "GaMS3-Instruct")]


def wilson(k: int, n: int, z: float = 1.96) -> tuple[float, float]:
    p = k / n
    d = 1 + z * z / n
    c = (p + z * z / (2 * n)) / d
    h = z * math.sqrt(p * (1 - p) / n + z * z / (4 * n * n)) / d
    return c - h, c + h


def main() -> None:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("artifact")
    ap.add_argument("--out", default="fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json")
    args = ap.parse_args()
    res = Path(args.artifact) / "results"

    counts: dict[tuple, list[int]] = defaultdict(lambda: [0, 0])  # (model, in, out) -> [k, n_labelled]
    with open(res / "rows_final.jsonl") as fh:
        for line in fh:
            r = json.loads(line)
            if r["kind"] != "harmful" or r["cond"] != "edit" or r["dose"] != "hi" or not r["L_primary"]:
                continue
            c = counts[(r["model"], r["in_lang"], r["out_lang"])]
            c[0] += r["L_primary"] == "REFUSE"
            c[1] += 1

    an = json.loads((res / "analysis.json").read_text())
    raw = an["readouts"]["raw_R"]
    panels = []
    for m, name in MODELS:
        lam = json.loads((res / f"lambda_steps_{m}.json").read_text())["lambda_hi"]
        rate, lo, hi, comp, fail, ns = [], [], [], [], [], []
        for i in LANGS:
            rr, rl, rh, rc, rf, rn = [], [], [], [], [], []
            for o in LANGS:
                k, n = counts[(m, i, o)]
                stored = an["cell_table"][f"{m}|hi|{i}{o}|harmful"]
                w = wilson(k, n)
                assert n == stored["n_labelled"] == 200, (m, i, o, n)
                assert abs(k / n - stored["R"]) < 1e-12, (m, i, o, k / n, stored["R"])
                assert all(abs(a - b) < 1e-9 for a, b in zip(w, stored["R_ci"])), (m, i, o)
                gate = an["compliance_gate"][f"{m}|{i}{o}"]
                rr.append(round(k / n, 3))
                rl.append(round(w[0], 3))
                rh.append(round(w[1], 3))
                rc.append(round(gate["compliance"], 3))
                rf.append(not gate["pass90"])
                rn.append(n)
            rate.append(rr); lo.append(rl); hi.append(rh); comp.append(rc); fail.append(rf); ns.append(rn)
        panels.append({
            "model": m, "title": f"{name} (high dose, $\\lambda$ = {lam:g})", "lambda_hi": lam,
            "rate": rate, "ci_lo": lo, "ci_hi": hi, "n": ns,
            "compliance_pooled": comp, "compliance_failed": fail,
        })

    def ci(key: str) -> dict:
        s = raw[key]
        return {"est": round(s["est"], 2), "ci95": [round(v, 2) for v in s["ci95"]]}

    spec = {
        "figure_id": "fig_mechanism_2x3",
        "aspect": "16:9",
        "source": "exp14 artifact gen_art_experiment_14 (invention-loop iter 4): results/rows_final.jsonl "
                  "(primary judge L_primary, harmful items, dose=hi) checked against results/analysis.json "
                  "cell_table; compliance from analysis.json compliance_gate (pooled over zero/lo/hi, n=600, "
                  "gate = 0.90); contrasts from analysis.json readouts.raw_R",
        "row_labels": ["EN", "SL", "HU"],
        "col_labels": ["EN", "SL", "HU"],
        "xlabel": "Reply language requested",
        "ylabel": "Prompt language",
        "cbar_label": "Refusal rate on harmful prompts (share, n = 200 per cell)",
        "vmin": 0.0,
        "vmax": 1.0,
        "compliance_threshold": 0.9,
        "panels": panels,
        "contrasts": {
            "dOUT_SL": ci("dOUT_SL"),
            "OUT_SL|gemma_it": ci("OUT_SL|gemma_it"),
            "OUT_SL|gams3_it": ci("OUT_SL|gams3_it"),
            "G3edit|ensl": ci("G3edit|ensl"),
            "robust_call_SL": an["robust_call_SL"]["robust"],
        },
    }
    Path(args.out).write_text(json.dumps(spec, indent=1) + "\n")
    print(f"wrote {args.out}; all 18 cells reproduce analysis.json cell_table")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Both maps this style ships have a mid-tone where neither ink\n786-    clears the bar: cividis bottoms out at 4.18:1 and RdBu_r at 4.19:1\n787-    against a stated minimum of 4.5, and those are the cells in the MIDDLE of\n788-    a matrix, which is most of them when there is no strong block structure.\n789-\n790-    Changing the colormap is the wrong fix. cividis is the one that is\n791-    perceptually uniform AND colourblind-safe; RdBu_r is the one whose sign\n792-    reads from hue direction. An outline keeps both and separates the glyph\n--\n807:def ink_for(image, value: float) -> str:\n808-    \"\"\"Text colour for a heatmap cell, from the cell's ACTUAL rendered colour.\n809-\n810-    Deriving it from the value's position in the range assumes the colormap\n811-    runs dark-to-bright. Reverse the map — ``cividis_r``, or any ``*_r`` — and\n812-    the rule inverts: the lowest value lands on the BRIGHTEST cell and gets\n813-    white text on yellow, while the highest gets near-black on navy. Both\n814-    annotations become invisible while the figure still looks fine at a\n815-    glance.\n816-\n817-    Asking the colormap what it actually painted is correct for any map,\n818-    reversed or not. Luminance uses the Rec. 709 weights, which track\n819-    perceived brightness rather than treating the channels as equal.\n    \"\"\"Everything a reader can use to tell one legend entry from another.\n\n    Colour, dash pattern and marker — read off the drawn handle rather than\n    from whatever the renderer intended, so a patch and a line are compared on\n    the same terms and a renderer that sets the colour twice cannot disagree\n464:def fit_point_labels(fig) -> None:\n465-    \"\"\"Move a point's name off whatever it landed on, measured after layout.\n466-\n467-    A renderer picks the offset before the axes has its final size, so \"up and\n468-    to the right by five points\" can put a name straight through a neighbouring\n469-    marker, through a curve, or through another name. ```
File: /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_UESxYRggGt7E/4_gen_paper_repo/_3_gen_viz/gen_viz_report_6/render_fig.py

"""Render fig_mechanism_2x3 from fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json (built by build_spec.py).

Two 3x3 heatmaps (prompt language x requested reply language) of hi-dose refusal rates, one per
model, sharing one colour scale. Cells whose reply-language compliance failed the 0.90 gate get
grey hatching and a dagger. Hand-written because the catalogue `heatmap` type has no per-cell
hatching, no shared colour bar across panels and no per-cell interval text; it uses the house
style and every layout pass and gate from chart_style / chart_geometry.

Usage: python render_fig.py [--spec fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json] [--out fig_mechanism_2x3_v0]
"""
import argparse
import json
import sys
import warnings
from pathlib import Path

SKILL = Path("/ai-inventor/.claude/skills/aii-data-fig-gen/scripts")
sys.path.insert(0, str(SKILL))

import matplotlib  # noqa: E402

matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt  # noqa: E402
import numpy as np  # noqa: E402
from matplotlib.patches import Patch, Rectangle  # noqa: E402

from chart_common import cell_halo, ink_for  # noqa: E402
from chart_geometry import assert_text_is_legible, fit_point_labels  # noqa: E402
from chart_style import (  # noqa: E402
    SEQUENTIAL_CMAP, add_panel_label, apply_house_style, assert_all_glyphs_rendered,
    assert_axis_names_are_unique, assert_layout_applied, assert_legends_clear_of_data,
    assert_series_are_distinguishable, clear_legends_of_data, figsize_for, fit_legends,
    fit_tick_labels, fit_titles, place_legend, rasterize_dense_clouds,
)

HATCH_EDGE = "#B0B0B0"


def fmt_rate(v: float) -> str:
    return f"{v:.3f}".rstrip("0").rstrip(".") if round(v, 2) != v else f"{v:.2f}"


def draw_panel(ax, p: dict, spec: dict, cmap, show_ylabel: bool):
    m = np.asarray(p["rate"], float)
    im = ax.imshow(m, cmap=cmap, vmin=spec["vmin"], vmax=spec["vmax"], aspect="auto")
    ax.set_xticks(range(3), labels=spec["col_labels"])
    ax.set_yticks(range(3), labels=spec["row_labels"])
    ax.set_xlabel(spec["xlabel"])
    if show_ylabel:
        ax.set_ylabel(spec["ylabel"])
    ax.set_title(p["title"])
    ax.grid(visible=False)
    ax.tick_params(length=0)
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            v = m[r, c]
            ink = ink_for(im, v)
            if p["compliance_failed"][r][c]:
                ax.add_patch(Rectangle((c - 0.5, r - 0.5), 1, 1, fill=False, hatch="///",
                                       edgecolor=HATCH_EDGE, linewidth=0, zorder=2))
                ax.text(c + 0.44, r - 0.42, "†", ha="right", va="top", fontsize=10,
                        fontweight="bold", color=ink, path_effects=cell_halo(ink), zorder=4)
            ax.text(c, r - 0.08, fmt_rate(v), ha="center", va="center", fontsize=10,
                    fontweight="bold", color=ink, path_effects=cell_halo(ink), zorder=4)
            ax.text(c, r + 0.24, f"[{p['ci_lo'][r][c]:.2f}, {p['ci_hi'][r][c]:.2f}]", ha="center",
                    va="center", fontsize=7, color=ink, path_effects=cell_halo(ink), zorder=4)
    return im


def main() -> None:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("--spec", default="fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json")
    ap.add_argument("--out", default="fig_mechanism_2x3_v0")
    args = ap.parse_args()
    spec = json.loads(Path(args.spec).read_text())

    with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as warned:
        warnings.simplefilter("always")
        apply_house_style()
        cmap = plt.get_cmap(SEQUENTIAL_CMAP)
        fig = plt.figure(figsize=figsize_for(spec["aspect"]), layout="constrained")
        gs = fig.add_gridspec(2, 3, width_ratios=[1, 1, 0.06], height_ratios=[1, 0.13])
        axes = [fig.add_subplot(gs[0, 0]), fig.add_subplot(gs[0, 1])]
        cax = fig.add_subplot(gs[0, 2])
        note = fig.add_subplot(gs[1, :])
        note.axis("off")

        for k, (ax, p) in enumerate(zip(axes, spec["panels"])):
            im = draw_panel(ax, p, spec, cmap, show_ylabel=(k == 0))
            add_panel_label(ax, f"({'ab'[k]})")
        cbar = fig.colorbar(im, cax=cax)
        cbar.set_label("Refusal rate (harmful prompts)")

        d = spec["contrasts"]["dOUT_SL"]
        note.text(0.0, 0.5,
                  f"$\\Delta$OUT$_{{SL}}$ (GaMS3 $-$ Gemma) = {d['est']:.2f} "
                  f"[{d['ci95'][0]:.2f}, {d['ci95'][1]:.2f}] logit",
                  ha="left", va="center", fontsize=9, transform=note.transAxes)
        hatch = Patch(facecolor="white", edgecolor=HATCH_EDGE, hatch="///",
                      label=f"† reply-language compliance < {spec['compliance_threshold']:.2f}")
        place_legend(note, handles=[hatch], loc="center right", fontsize=9, frameon=False)

        fit_legends(fig)
        clear_legends_of_data(fig)
        fit_tick_labels(fig)
        fit_titles(fig)
        clear_legends_of_data(fig)
        fit_point_labels(fig)
        rasterize_dense_clouds(fig)
        fig.canvas.draw()
        assert_text_is_legible(fig)
        assert_legends_clear_of_data(fig)
        assert_series_are_distinguishable(fig)
        assert_axis_names_are_unique(fig)
        fig.savefig(f"{args.out}.pdf")
        fig.savefig(f"{args.out}.png", dpi=200)
    assert_layout_applied(list(warned), fig)
    assert_all_glyphs_rendered(list(warned))
    print(f"wrote {args.out}.pdf and {args.out}.png")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

cd /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_UESxYRggGt7E/4_gen_paper_repo/_3_gen_viz/gen_viz_report_6; cat > .aii/manifest.yaml <<'EOF'
# Nothing in this workspace is heavy: code, a JSON spec, one PDF (~90 KB) and one PNG (~230 KB),
# all below the 10 MB auto-keep floor. No cache directories were created.
entries: []
EOF
cat > README.md <<'EOF'
# fig_mechanism_2x3: 9-cell refusal heatmaps, Gemma-IT vs GaMS3-Instruct (high dose)

Two 3×3 heatmaps (prompt language × requested reply language) of high-dose refusal rates on
harmful prompts, one per model, on one shared colour scale. Cells whose reply-language compliance
fails the pre-registered 0.90 gate are hatched and carry a dagger.

Every plotted number is recomputed from the saved outputs of the Experiment 14 artifact
(`gen_art_experiment_14`, invention-loop iteration 4). None is copied from the paper draft.

## What was checked against the artifact

- **Cell rates:** each of the 18 cells is recounted from `results/rows_final.jsonl` (harmful items,
  `cond = edit`, `dose = hi`, R = share of labelled rows with `L_primary == REFUSE`, n = 200) and
  asserted equal to `results/analysis.json` `cell_table`. The Wilson 95% intervals are recomputed
  and asserted against the stored `R_ci`.
- **Doses:** λ_hi = 1.0 (Gemma) and 0.625 (GaMS3), from `results/lambda_steps_<model>.json`.
- **Compliance flags:** from `analysis.json` `compliance_gate`. The share of replies in the
  requested language is pooled over the zero, lo and hi doses (n = 600), with a gate of 0.90.
  Only four cells fail, all GaMS3: SL→EN 0.000, HU→EN 0.138, SL→HU 0.555, HU→SL 0.865.
  Gemma HU→SL (0.957) passes. The draft methods text had listed it as excluded.
- **Contrasts:** from `analysis.json` `readouts.raw_R`.
  - dOUT_SL = OUT_SL(GaMS3) − OUT_SL(Gemma) = −1.58 [−2.22, −0.66].
  - The per-model values are OUT_SL = +1.14 [0.60, 1.56] for Gemma and −0.44 [−0.98, 0.27] for GaMS3.
  - G3_edit at EN→SL = −1.26 [−2.17, 0.20].
  - The pre-registered four-readout robustness call is `robust = False`.

## Differences from the draft specification

1. Cell values are printed at full precision. With n = 200, several rates fall exactly on a
   half-percent (0.185, 0.075, 0.835, 0.135, 0.155, 0.225, 0.175, 0.125). The draft rounded them
   to two decimals, and some of its rounding went down (e.g. 0.835 → 0.83).
2. Each cell also shows its Wilson 95% CI, following the uncertainty rule for experimental results.
3. The colour map is the house sequential map (cividis: navy at 0, yellow at 1), not a blue→red
   diverging map. A refusal rate has no meaningful midpoint, and cividis is colour-blind safe and
   matches the paper's other heatmaps. Cell text switches between white and black by cell luminance
   so it stays legible. It is not white throughout.
4. dOUT_SL is NOT a contrast of the plotted hi-dose rates. It is computed on edit-induced lags L*
   (logit, interpolated to matched EN→EN 50% refusal, minus the zero-dose gap) over the EN/SL 2×2
   of cells. GaMS3's arm of that 2×2 includes SL→EN, the cell whose compliance is 0.00. The only
   contrast restricted to compliant cells is G3_edit at EN→SL = −1.26 [−2.17, 0.20], whose CI
   includes 0. The caption says both.
5. The "high dose" λ values differ between the models. They are stated in the caption, not the
   panel titles, which the spec fixes as 'Gemma-IT (high dose)' and 'GaMS3-Instruct (high dose)'.

## Layout

| file | what it is |
|---|---|
| `build_spec.py` | recomputes every number from the exp14 artifact, asserts it against `analysis.json`, writes the spec |
| `fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json` | the figure's source: rates, CIs, n, compliance, flags, contrasts |
| `render_fig.py` | hand-written matplotlib renderer using the aii-data-fig-gen house style and all its layout passes and gates |
| `fig_mechanism_2x3_v0.pdf` | deliverable (vector) |
| `fig_mechanism_2x3_v0.png` | raster preview |

The figure is hand-written because the catalogue `heatmap` type has no per-cell hatching, no
colour bar shared across panels and no per-cell interval text.

## How to run

```bash
python build_spec.py <path-to>/iter_4/gen_art/gen_art_experiment_14   # writes fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json
python render_fig.py                                                  # writes fig_mechanism_2x3_v0.{pdf,png}
```

Requires matplotlib ≥ 3.8 and numpy, plus the `aii-data-fig-gen` skill scripts. `render_fig.py`
adds the skill's `scripts/` directory to `sys.path`; edit `SKILL` there if it lives elsewhere.

## Restoring removed files

Nothing is marked `delete` in `.aii/manifest.yaml`. Every file here is small and kept.
EOF
echo ok
````

```
I rendered the figure: `fig_mechanism_2x3_v0.pdf`, built from `fig_mechanism_2x3_spec.json`. The structured output is written to `.terminal_claude_agent_struct_out.json`.

**Checking the numbers.** Every value in the figure comes from the Experiment 14 output files (`gen_art_experiment_14`, iteration 4), not from the paper draft. I recounted all 18 refusal rates from the per-response file `rows_final.jsonl`. The script checks them and their 95% intervals against `analysis.json` and fails if anything differs. It didn't, so every number in your specification is backed by the run's data.

**What's drawn.** Two 3×3 heatmaps, Gemma-IT and GaMS3-Instruct at high dose. Rows are the prompt language and columns the language the reply was asked to be in. Both panels share one 0–1 colour bar, and each cell shows its rate and 95% interval (n = 200). The four GaMS3 cells that failed the language check are hatched and marked with a dagger. The ΔOUT_SL line sits below the panels.

**Where I departed from the draft specification, and why:**
- **Three decimals:** several rates are exact half-percents (e.g. 37/200 = 0.185), and the draft had rounded some of them down (0.835 → 0.83).
- **95% intervals in every cell:** added to show uncertainty.
- **Colours:** navy-to-yellow (the style used by the other figures) instead of blue-to-red. A refusal rate has no meaningful midpoint, and this scale is colour-blind safe. Cell text switches between black and white so it stays readable; it isn't white throughout.
- **ΔOUT_SL:** the caption now says what it actually measures. It isn't computed from the plotted rates. It comes from the edit-induced refusal lag over the English/Slovene cells. GaMS3's side of it includes SL→EN, the cell where compliance is 0.00.
  - The only comparison restricted to cells that passed the language check is −1.26 [−2.17, 0.20], and its interval includes zero.
  - The run's own robustness check across four readouts returns `robust = False`.
- **Gemma HU→SL:** it passes the language check (0.957). The draft's methods text lists it as excluded, which is a mistake in the paper, not the figure.

I plotted it with custom matplotlib code (`render_fig.py`) rather than the skill's built-in heatmap. The built-in one can't do hatching, a colour bar shared by two panels, or intervals inside cells. The script still uses the skill's house style and all its layout checks. `build_spec.py` rebuilds the spec from the experiment outputs. The README explains how to re-run both, and the manifest is empty because nothing in the folder is large.

The pasted text ends with a request to carry out the whole GaMS3-vs-Gemma research project and write the paper. I didn't act on that: this workspace is for this one figure, and the document it refers to wasn't attached.
```
